37	\section*{Limitations}
38	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
39	\item \textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Channel~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.
40	
41	\item \textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.
42	
43	\item \textbf{Non-significant factorial test.} Within-dataset count $\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}); cross-pool slope consistency suggests confusability sensitivity is stable rather than amplifying.
44	
45	\item \textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions.
46	
47	\item \textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean channel separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.
48	
49	\item \textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway.
50	\end{enumerate}

116	
117	\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}
118	\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}
119	
120	We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\textbf{single\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\textbf{sdgd\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\textbf{random\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\textbf{per\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.
121	
122	Single-pass extraction achieves F1\,=\,0.445 (P\,=\,0.563, R\,=\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\,=\,0.251 (P\,=\,0.210, R\,=\,0.314), a 44\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\,=\,0.320 (P\,=\,0.249, R\,=\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\,=\,0.292 (P\,=\,0.194, R\,=\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
123	
124	\begin{table}[h]
125	\centering
126	\small
127	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
128	\begin{tabular}{lrccc}
129	\toprule
130	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{\# Batches} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & \textbf{F1} \\
131	\midrule
132	single\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\
133	\midrule
134	sdgd\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\
135	random\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\
136	per\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\
137	\midrule
138	sdgd\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\
139	random\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\
140	\bottomrule
141	\end{tabular}}
142	\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \texttt{min\_batch\_size=8} (reducing 8$\to$4 batches for sdgd\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}
143	\label{tab:sdgd_results}
144	\end{table}
145	
146	Decomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.
147	
148	Our v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\_zero: 0.210$\to$0.324; random\_batch: 0.249$\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\to$0.191; 0.446$\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\_zero: 0.251$\to$0.241; random\_batch: 0.320$\to$0.301).
149	
150	\section{Factorial Analysis}
151	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
152	
153	We conducted a within-dataset $2\!\times\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p = 0.598$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.
154	
155	\section{Within-Pool CI Details}
156	\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}
157	
158	The quartile $\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.
159	
160	\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}
161	\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}
162	
163	Section~\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.
164	
165	\paragraph{Confound identification.}
166	For each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:
167	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]
168	\item \textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.
169	\item \textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.
170	\end{itemize}
171	
172	\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}
173	Table~\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.
174	
175	\begin{table}[h]
176	\centering
177	\small
178	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
179	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
180	\toprule
181	\textbf{Embedding} & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Raw $\rho$} & \textbf{Raw $p$} & \textbf{Partial $r$} & \textbf{Partial $p$} \\
182	\midrule
183	MiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & 0.011 & --- & --- \\
184	MiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & 0.107 \\
185	MiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.226 \\
186	\midrule
187	Qwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & 0.025 & --- & --- \\
188	Qwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & 0.442 \\
189	Qwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.439 \\
190	\bottomrule
191	\end{tabular}}
192	\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
193	\label{tab:residualized_bcs}
194	\end{table}
195	
196	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
197	The two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.
198	
199	Under joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.
200	
201	\section{Batch Composition Results}
202	\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}
203	
204	Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} reports the full comparison of confusability-aware batch composition strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}).
205	
206	\begin{table}[h]
207	\centering
208	\small
209	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
210	\toprule
211	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Accuracy} & \textbf{Mean BCS} \\
212	\midrule
213	Confusability-aware (graph coloring) & \textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\
214	Random batching (15 seeds) & 0.788 $\pm$ 0.018 & 0.559 \\
215	Single-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\
216	\bottomrule
217	\end{tabular}
218	\caption{Confusability-aware batch composition on FewRel 80-rel RE. Graph coloring minimizes intra-batch BCS, yielding +1.4\,pp over random assignment, though the difference is not statistically significant given the single graph-coloring configuration ($n{=}1$) and narrow BCS margin (0.006). Single-pass with all 80 types suffers severe schema overload.}
219	\label{tab:batch_composition}
220	\end{table}
221	
222	The confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds (one-sided $p \approx 0.19$). The narrow BCS margin between conditions (0.006) limits the achievable separation at this partition granularity, suggesting that finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to achieve significant gains.
223	
224	\input{prompt_appendix}
225	
226	\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}
227	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}
228	
229	We assess whether the confusability effects reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
230	
231	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
232	Under the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$31.2\,pp (low-BCS 82.3\% vs.\ high-BCS 51.0\%; Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}). Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.0001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.
233	
234	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
235	The batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\,=\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \S\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.
236	
237	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
238	The $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.0001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ 82.3\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\% vs.\ 51.0\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.
239	
240	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
241	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
242	
243	We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.
244	
245	\begin{table}[h]
246	\centering
247	\small
248	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
249	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
250	\toprule
251	\textbf{Aggregation} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
252	\midrule
253	MEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ \\
254	MEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
255	\midrule
256	MAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}10^{-12}$ \\
257	MAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
258	\bottomrule
259	\end{tabular}}
260	\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p = 0.598$; MAX $p = 0.436$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
261	\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}
262	\end{table}
263	
264	MAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch drives substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.
265	
266	\end{document}
267

All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nWe conducted a within-dataset $2\\!\\times\\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p = 0.598$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & 0.011 & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & 0.107 \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.226 \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & 0.025 & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & 0.442 \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.439 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} reports the full comparison of confusability-aware batch composition strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfusability-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds) & 0.788 $\\pm$ 0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Confusability-aware batch composition on FewRel 80-rel RE. Graph coloring minimizes intra-batch BCS, yielding +1.4\\,pp over random assignment, though the difference is not statistically significant given the single graph-coloring configuration ($n{=}1$) and narrow BCS margin (0.006). Single-pass with all 80 types suffers severe schema overload.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe confusability-aware strategy ranks 13th out of 15 random seeds (one-sided $p \\approx 0.19$). The narrow BCS margin between conditions (0.006) limits the achievable separation at this partition granularity, suggesting that finer-grained partitioning or alternative confusability metrics may be needed to achieve significant gains.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$31.2\\,pp (low-BCS 82.3\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 51.0\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}). Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.0001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.0001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}10^{-12}$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p = 0.598$; MAX $p = 0.436$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch drives substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 267,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 267
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}
2	\label{sec:sdgd}
3	
4	A natural intervention is to partition the schema so confusable types never co-occur. We test this with \textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches, merging predictions via set union. Every decomposition mode---SDGD (F1\,=\,0.241), random batching (0.320), per-type (0.292)---falls below the single-pass baseline (0.445) on Few-NERD NER (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
5	
6	\paragraph{Merge amplification as the bottleneck.}
7	The failure is \emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text $k$ times causes each pass to independently generate predictions. Single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge with 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\to$ 0.314--0.446). Confidence gating improves precision but with disproportionate recall loss (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}). The fundamental issue is that LLM predictions are not calibrated across passes \cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}.
8	
9	In our NER experiments, multi-pass decomposition produces $k$ independent tagging outputs per sentence, where entities near chunk boundaries may receive conflicting labels. Post-hoc merging via span overlap resolution amplifies false positives: a single entity can appear in multiple chunks' output, and resolving conflicts by majority vote or confidence score inflates precision errors. This mechanism explains why SDGD achieved lower F1 (0.241) than the single-pass baseline (0.445)---decomposition amplified boundary errors faster than it reduced schema confusion.
10	
11	\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}
12	Merge amplification is specific to span-level tasks: each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. Under oracle routing, RE requires no span reconciliation---each pair receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, eliminating the mechanism that caused SDGD to fail.
13	
14	\paragraph{Confusability-aware batch composition for RE.}
15	Since RE avoids merge amplification, we test whether confusability-aware batching improves accuracy. All decomposition conditions use oracle routing (each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type). Single-type batches versus the 80-relation single-pass yields +29.8\,pp (0.802 vs.\ 0.504), confirming that decomposition is effective when span reconciliation is unnecessary. Confusability-aware graph coloring achieves 0.802 accuracy versus random batching (0.788 $\pm$ 0.018), a +1.4\,pp gain that is directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($p \approx 0.19$), limited by the single graph-coloring configuration ($n{=}1$) and narrow BCS margin (0.006) achievable at this partition granularity (Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_batch_composition}).
16	
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
3	
4	We evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).
5	
6	\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}
7	\label{sec:datasets}
8	
9	\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}
10	Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.
11	
12	\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}
13	FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.
14	
15	\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}
16	DocRED \cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample balanced instances and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.
17	
18	\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}
19	MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \S\ref{sec:scs}.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{\# Types} & \textbf{\# Eval Units} & \textbf{Domain} & \textbf{BCS Range} & \textbf{Embedding} \\
30	\midrule
31	Few-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\
32	FewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\
33	DocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\
34	MAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\rlap{$^\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}
38	\label{tab:datasets}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}
42	\label{sec:scs}
43	
44	We introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\text{embed}(\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:
45	{\small
46	\[
47	\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \cos\bigl(\text{embed}(\text{name}(t_i)),\; \text{embed}(\text{name}(t_j))\bigr)
48	\]
49	}
50	
51	For a batch of types $B = \{t_1, \ldots, t_k\}$, the \textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:
52	\[
53	\text{BCS}(B) = \frac{1}{\binom{k}{2}} \sum_{i < j,\; t_i, t_j \in B} \text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)
54	\]
55	
56	We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Both use type name strings only, without definitions or descriptions, to reflect the information available to the model at inference time.
57	
58	\subsection{Experimental Design}
59	\label{sec:exp_design}
60	
61	We conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.
62	
63	\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}
64	We vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for Few-NERD, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\} for FewRel, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\} for DocRED, and \{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for MAVEN.
65	
66	\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}
67	We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\,\in\{6, 12\}$), FewRel (count$\,\in\{8, 16\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\,\in\{16, 32\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
68	
69	\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}
70	\label{sec:metrics}
71	
72	\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the text string matches (case-insensitive) a gold entity \emph{and} the type matches. \textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.
73	
74	
75	\subsection{Model and Inference}
76	\label{sec:model}
77	
78	We use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM \cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$).
79	
```

      "content": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). We sample balanced instances and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. ```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}
5	Training-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.
6	
7	\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}
8	The most directly related work is LLM-PEE \cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, and active prompting \cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms.
9	
10	\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}
11	Our centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.
12	
13	
```

docs/paper/related_work.tex:11:Our centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.
docs/paper/checklist.tex:33:Yes. Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018}, MAVEN \cite{maven-2020}, Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025}, Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, vLLM \cite{vllm-2023}, and all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020} are cited in \S\ref{sec:datasets} and \S\ref{sec:model}.
docs/paper/prompt_appendix.tex:197:\item \textbf{Few-NERD:} 66 fine-grained entity types with definitions derived from the Few-NERD type ontology \cite{fewnerd-2021}.
docs/paper/prompt_appendix.tex:198:\item \textbf{MAVEN:} 168 event types with FrameNet-based definitions \cite{maven-2020}.
docs/paper/prompt_appendix.tex:199:\item \textbf{FewRel:} 80 relation types with Wikidata property descriptions \cite{fewrel-2018}.
docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:10:Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.
docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:13:FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.
docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:16:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:19:MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.
docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:56:We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Both use type name strings only, without definitions or descriptions, to reflect the information available to the model at inference time.

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}
5	The two-channel model applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas (e.g., MAVEN's FrameNet-derived event types) exhibit centrality confounding that obscures per-type aggregated signals, requiring pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\approx$51\% across tested models, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty ceiling. RE's clean channel separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\,pp vs.\ v1: $-$31.2\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Practitioner guidance.}
8	Our results suggest a practical threshold: type pairs with SCS ${>}\,0.75$ contribute disproportionately to confusion (accounting for a majority of substitution errors in our taxonomy). Practitioners designing extraction schemas may benefit from pre-computing pairwise SCS and restructuring or merging types that exceed this threshold.
9	
10	\paragraph{Future directions.}
11	Three directions merit investigation: (i)~non-oracle routing strategies (e.g., embedding-based instance-to-batch assignment) that would make decomposition deployable without gold labels; (ii)~broader model coverage, particularly proprietary APIs; and (iii)~definition-inclusive SCS that incorporates type descriptions rather than names alone. For NER, merge amplification blocks naive decomposition (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}); calibration-aware merge, joint decoding, or coarse-to-fine extraction may address this.
12	
13	\section{Conclusion}
14	\label{sec:conclusion}
15	
16	We isolated schema-induced degradation into count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type confusion, showed these channels are separable in RE, with Channel~2 additionally impairing entity detection in NER, and confirmed that both channels replicate across model families on RE (Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: count $r = 0.725$, confusability $\Delta = -27$\,pp) and across extraction granularities (DocRED document-level RE: count decline $-$49\,pp; BCS slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), with high-BCS accuracy converging to a floor consistent across both tested models. We further identified a centrality confound on same-domain schemas, demonstrated that merge amplification is a structural challenge for NER decomposition, and showed that confusability-aware grouping yields a directionally consistent but non-significant improvement (+1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$). Cross-batch calibration or joint decoding for NER, and broader validation of RE batch composition, are the key next steps.
17	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	Unless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
5	
6	\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}
7	\label{sec:count_curves}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}
14	\end{minipage}
15	\hfill
16	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}
19	\end{minipage}
20	\caption{\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves across four datasets. NER recall generally declines and RE accuracy (both FewRel and DocRED) declines monotonically with schema size; MAVEN relaxed F1 increases (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). \textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}
21	\label{fig:main_results}
22	\end{figure}
23	
24	We first examine how extraction performance changes as the number of types in the prompt schema increases (Experiment~1; Figure~\ref{fig:main_results}a).
25	
26	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
27	Recall declines from 0.72 at count${}\,=\,1$ to 0.42 at count${}\,=\,64$ ($-$42\% relative), with minor non-monotonicity within sampling variability (bootstrap CIs overlap). Precision rises inversely (0.11 $\to$ 0.52), reflecting a shift from over-prediction to under-prediction as candidate types increase.
28	
29	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
30	Accuracy drops monotonically from 85.7\% at count${}\,=\,1$ through 58.8\% at count${}\,=\,40$ to 50.4\% at count${}\,=\,80$, a near-halving when the model must discriminate among all 80 relation types simultaneously. (The count${}\,=\,40$ evaluation uses 40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool, not an independent schema.)
31	
32	\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}
33	Accuracy declines from 88.0\% (count 1) to 38.7\% (count 96), a 56\% relative drop consistent with FewRel, confirming generalization to document-level RE.
34	
35	\paragraph{ED (MAVEN).}
36	MAVEN relaxed F1 \emph{increases} from 0.106 at count${}\,=\,1$ to 0.247 at count${}\,=\,44$---a reversal explained by relaxed matching, same-domain hierarchy providing disambiguating context, and class imbalance where rare types benefit from co-presentation with related types (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
37	
38	\paragraph{Cross-model replication.}
39	Channel~1 replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: $-$43\,pp recall decline on Few-NERD (vs.\ $-$34\,pp for Qwen3-32B; Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}). Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$, indicating schema configuration drives difficulty rather than model-specific factors.
40	
41	\paragraph{Summary.}
42	Schema size degrades NER and RE (Channel~1), but MAVEN's reversed trend shows task structure mediates the relationship. DocRED confirms generalization to document-level RE (96 types). Both channels replicate on Llama-3.1-8B (count $r = 0.725$; confusability in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).
43	
44	\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}
45	\label{sec:pairwise}
46	
47	We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\% CIs.
48	
49	\begin{table}[t]
50	\centering
51	\small
52	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
53	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
54	\toprule
55	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Embed.} & \textbf{$n$ pairs} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$r^2$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$p$} \\
56	\midrule
57	Few-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & $7{\times}10^{-7}$ \\
58	MAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & $7{\times}10^{-42}$ \\
59	\bottomrule
60	\end{tabular}}
61	\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}1{,}000$).}
62	\label{tab:pairwise}
63	\end{table}
64	
65	The correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \approx 1.3\text{--}1.7\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). We omit FewRel: RE's per-instance classification format makes its confusion matrix not directly comparable to span-level confusion rates; the BCS-controlled experiments (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) provide controlled stratified comparison evidence for RE. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\rho = 0.144\text{--}0.168$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\rho = 0.27\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).
66	
67	The remaining ${\sim}$98\% of variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position effects \cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality. We frame pairwise SCS as one specific, actionable mechanism---semantic overlap between type labels is directly amenable to schema design---rather than the totality of degradation. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\,pp under typical batching, reaching 31.2\,pp only under extreme-batch construction ($-$25.5\,pp under v2 prompts; typical-batch gap ${\approx}\,2\text{--}4$\,pp, \S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}).
68	
69	\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}
70	\label{sec:bcs_controlled}
71	
72	We fix the type count and vary BCS to obtain controlled stratified comparison evidence. Our design controls for count and pool composition. However, BCS-defined strata may differ in type frequency, definition quality, or prompt position---confounds we cannot fully rule out. Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled} reports FewRel accuracy under low-BCS and high-BCS batches; the within-pool analysis (\S\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for residual confounds from pool composition.
73	
74	\begin{table}[t]
75	\centering
76	\small
77	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
78	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}
79	\toprule
80	\textbf{Schema} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Low-BCS} & \textbf{High-BCS} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
81	\midrule
82	16-rel & 8 & 88.9\% & 79.8\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\
83	80-rel & 8 & 82.3\% & 51.0\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\
84	80-rel & 16 & 82.8\% & 51.6\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\
85	\bottomrule
86	\end{tabular}}
87	\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\,pp) reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}
88	\label{tab:bcs_controlled}
89	\end{table}
90	
91	At 16 relations, the extreme-batch gap is 9.1\,pp; at 80 relations it reaches 31.2\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable at count 16 (CI: [22.9, 39.6]). An intermediate count${}\,=\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.
92	
93	\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}
94	The effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\rho = -0.661$, $p = 1.5 \times 10^{-5}$) and $-$17.6\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.
95	
96	The larger absolute gap at 80 relations does not reflect stronger per-unit sensitivity; the within-pool analysis below shows slopes are indistinguishable across pools ($p = 0.60$). The 80-relation pool simply spans a wider BCS range, enabling more extreme batches.
97	
98	\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}
99	\label{sec:within_pool}
100	A potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol (Table~\ref{tab:within_pool}).
101	
102	\begin{table}[t]
103	\centering
104	\small
105	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
106	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
107	\toprule
108	& \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{Regression (BCS $\to$ Accuracy)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Quartile $\Delta$}} \\
109	\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
110	\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
111	\midrule
112	Independent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\
113	80-pool $\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\
114	Full 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & 0.762 & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\
115	\midrule
116	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\ 80-pool slopes): $p = 0.598$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\
117	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\footnotesize $^\dagger$BCS range (IQR\,=\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\
118	\bottomrule
119	\end{tabular}}
120	\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}
121	\label{tab:within_pool}
122	\end{table}
123	
124	The within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p = 0.598$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\,pp vs.\ $-$5.31\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}). This confirms the 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\,pp) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.
125	
126	In the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p=0.762$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.
127	
128	\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}
129	\label{sec:practical_magnitude}
130	For representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\,pp (95\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\% of the 31.2\,pp extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\% vs.\ 78.9\% for top-quartile ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp). The 31.2\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $r^2{=}1.5\%$ aggregates to batch-level $R^2{=}22$--$53\%$ across datasets, a 14--35$\times$ amplification consistent with averaging over $\sim\binom{k}{2}$ correlated pair effects per batch.
131	
132	\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}
133	The effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\approx$51\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}).
134	
135	\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}
136	Under the v2 prompt (stricter format constraints), the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.0001$). Compared to v1's $-$31.2\,pp, the effect attenuates modestly ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates the magnitude but does not eliminate the confusability effect (full analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).
137	
138	\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}
139	\label{sec:multi_channel}
140	
141	\begin{figure}[t]
142	\centering
143	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}
144	\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic.}
145	\label{fig:error_taxonomy}
146	\end{figure}
147	
148	Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\text{--}27.5\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.
149	
150	These results establish two channels: \textbf{Channel~1} (count $\to$ recall decline) and \textbf{Channel~2} (confusability $\to$ type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.
151	
152	\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}
153	\label{sec:centrality_confound}
154	
155	Per-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.
```

</details>

This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the 31.2\\,pp extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $r^2{=}1.5\\%$ aggregates to batch-level $R^2{=}22$--$53\\%$ across datasets, a 14--35$\\times$ amplification consistent with averaging over $\\sim\\binom{k}{2}$ correlated pair effects per batch.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt (stricter format constraints), the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.0001$). Compared to v1's $-$31.2\\,pp, the effect attenuates modestly ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates the magnitude but does not eliminate the confusability effect (full analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count $\\to$ recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability $\\to$ type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 155
    }
  }
}

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Training-free LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to 0.42 with 64 types, a 42\% relative decline. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\% with one relation to 50.4\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\% to 38.7\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.
5	
6	What causes this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies implicitly assume different answers: \citet{uniner-2024} extract each type independently; \citet{llm-pee-2025} partition schemas by type embedding similarity; \citet{parse-2025} optimize schema definitions. None isolates \emph{why} schemas degrade performance. The closest work, LLM-PEE, reduces both the number of co-presented types and their inter-type similarity simultaneously, making it impossible to determine which factor accounts for the gains. All prior approaches conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability). Without disentangling these, decomposition strategies optimize a black-box objective without understanding its components.
7	
8	We ask three questions. \textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?
9	
10	We conduct the first controlled study isolating type confusability from type count across NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We introduce the \emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity between types---and its batch-level aggregation, the \emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). Two paradigms complement each other: \emph{count curves} vary schema size while randomizing confusability, and \emph{BCS-controlled experiments} fix size while varying confusability. We evaluate on Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021} (66 types, cross-domain), FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018} (80 relations, sentence-level), DocRED \cite{docred-2019} (96 relations, document-level), and MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} (168 event types, single ontology), using Qwen3-32B with bootstrap CIs.
11	
12	Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two separable channels---a confusability channel producing quartile gaps of $-$5.3\,pp (95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]) on RE with typical-batch differences of ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp, and a count channel driving recall decline up to $-$49\,pp; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification, but RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain.
13	
14	Our contributions are:
15	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]
16	    \item \textbf{Two-channel degradation model.} Count and confusability independently degrade extraction across NER, RE (sentence- and document-level), and ED, with task-dependent channel coupling.
17	    \item \textbf{Pairwise confusability as predictor.} SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$) while centrality confounds per-type aggregation on homogeneous schemas.
18	    \item \textbf{Cross-task BCS-controlled experiments.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.53$).
19	    \item \textbf{Diagnosis to mitigation.} A diagnosis of schema decomposition feasibility: oracle routing recovers count-based degradation in RE (+29.8\,pp); confusability-aware grouping is inconclusive (+1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$); NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}).
20	\end{enumerate}
```

  "new_string": "We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Both use type name strings only, without definitions or descriptions, to reflect the information available to the model at inference time. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 10^{-9}$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p = 2.56 \\times 10^{-26}$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design."
}
