    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample balanced instances and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Both use type name strings only, without definitions or descriptions, to reflect the information available to the model at inference time.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the text string matches (case-insensitive) a gold entity \\emph{and} the type matches. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$).\n",

These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.",
    "newString": "For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}\nTraining-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \\cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \\cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \\cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \\cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \\cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n",

54	\]
55	
56	We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. Both use type name strings only, without definitions or descriptions, to reflect the information available to the model at inference time. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\,=\,0.034$ vs.\ MiniLM std${}\,=\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.
57	
58	\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}
59	SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 10^{-9}$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p = 2.56 \times 10^{-26}$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.
60	
61	\subsection{Experimental Design}
62	\label{sec:exp_design}
63	
64	We conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.
65	
66	\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}
67	We vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for Few-NERD, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\} for FewRel, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\} for DocRED, and \{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for MAVEN.
68

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HsgGqz4TR4GRAg6tSRF1mC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}
5	Training-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.
6	
7	\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}
8	The most directly related work is LLM-PEE \cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms.
9	
10	\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}
11	Our centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.
12	
13	
```
